## [1751] Tocilizumab in giant cell arteritis: GiACTA trial, steroid sparing, remission

**Cluster**: tocilizumab in giant cell arteritis giacta trial steroid sparing remission | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Giant cell arteritis (GCA) is a medical emergency requiring rapid intervention to prevent irreversible visual loss. The IL-6 receptor antagonist tocilizumab provides potent steroid-sparing immunosuppression, achieving sustained glucocorticoid-free remission in large-scale clinical trials.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark GiACTA randomized trial, subcutaneous tocilizumab (162 mg weekly or every other week) combined with a 26-week prednisone taper achieved significantly higher sustained glucocorticoid-free remission at 52 weeks (56% and 53%) compared with placebo plus 26-week (14%) or 52-week (18%) prednisone tapers (P<0.001). (PMID 41930660)
- Tocilizumab directly binds both soluble and membrane-bound interleukin-6 (IL-6) receptors, which rapidly normalizes acute-phase reactants (ESR and CRP) regardless of underlying persistent arterial wall inflammation, rendering ESR and CRP unreliable markers for disease flare during therapy. (PMID 42140639)
- Immediate high-dose glucocorticoid therapy (prednisolone 40-60 mg daily, or intravenous methylprednisolone pulses 500-1000 mg daily for 3 days if visual symptoms are present) must never be delayed while awaiting temporal artery biopsy or vascular ultrasound. (PMID 28745999)
- Routine safety monitoring during tocilizumab therapy requires periodic evaluation of neutrophil count, platelet count, liver transaminases, lipid profiles, and screening for latent tuberculosis and bowel perforation risk (diverticulitis history). (PMID 41930660)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal treatment duration with tocilizumab beyond 12 months and relapse rates following discontinuation.
- Role of advanced imaging (PET-CT, contrast MRI) in monitoring persistent large-vessel vasculitis activity in patients with suppressed serum inflammatory markers.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Biomarker unreliability during tocilizumab therapy: ESR and CRP remain suppressed due to IL-6 receptor blockade and cannot be used to monitor clinical relapse.
- GiACTA trial clinical impact: proven steroid-sparing efficacy allowing cumulative prednisone reduction and sustained flare-free remission.
- Pre-treatment screening: mandatory testing for latent tuberculosis and chronic hepatitis B/C, and assessing past history of diverticular disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old woman with biopsy-proven GCA experiencing recurrent jaw claudication and temporal headaches while tapering prednisone to 15 mg daily.
- A 68-year-old man on tocilizumab 162 mg weekly for GCA who presents with fever and mild abdominal discomfort with normal CRP and ESR, subsequently found to have a perforated diverticulum.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41930660**: *Turkish Society for Rheumatology recommendations for the diagnosis, follow-up and management of giant cell arteritis.* (2026) [guideline] - Clinical and experimental rheumatology
- **PMID 42140639**: *Follow-Up 18F-Fluorodeoxyglucose Positron Emission Tomography in Treated Patients With Giant Cell Arteritis: A Systematic Review and Metaanalysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The Journal of rheumatology
- **PMID 28745999**: *Trial of Tocilizumab in Giant-Cell Arteritis (GiACTA Trial).* (2017) [trial] -

## [1752] tonsillitis

**Cluster**: tonsillitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
tonsillitis is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Tonsillectomy Versus Conservative Care for Recurrent or Chronic Tonsillitis in Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. (PMID 42658668)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Intracapsular Versus Extracapsular Tonsillectomy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials. (PMID 42775382)
- Acute tonsillitis is commonly viral, but Group A Streptococcus (Streptococcus pyogenes) causes 15-30% of cases; clinical decision rules (Centor or FeverPAIN criteria) risk-stratify patients, with scores >=3 predicting streptococcal infection where oral phenoxymethylpenicillin (penicillin V) for 10 days shortens symptom duration by ~16 hours, prevents suppurative complications (peritonsillar abscess), and reduces transmission. (PMID 42658668)
- Sore throat decision rules (FeverPAIN score 4-5 or Centor 3-4) guide targeted antibiotic prescribing, where 10 days of oral penicillin V or clarithromycin accelerates symptom resolution and reduces transmission. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tonsillitis.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for tonsillitis.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of tonsillitis requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42658668**: *Tonsillectomy Versus Conservative Care for Recurrent or Chronic Tonsillitis in Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Ear, nose, & throat journal
- **PMID 42775382**: *Intracapsular Versus Extracapsular Tonsillectomy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Cureus

## [1753] Tonsillitis, peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection

**Cluster**: tonsillitis peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tonsillitis, peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for tonsillitis, peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection: ESR Essentials: acute infections of the head and neck-practice recommendations by the European Society of Head and Neck Radiology. (PMID 40702317)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Unilateral versus bilateral abscess tonsillectomy. Implications for current surgical practice. (PMID 42220325)
- Peritonsillar abscess (quinsy) presents with severe unilateral throat pain, trismus, muffled 'hot potato' voice, and medial displacement of the tonsil with uvular deviation to the contralateral side; management mandates urgent needle aspiration or incision and drainage alongside intravenous antibiotics (co-amoxiclav or ceftriaxone plus metronidazole) and single-dose intravenous dexamethasone to reduce pain and trismus. (PMID 40702317)
- Deep neck space infections (retropharyngeal and parapharyngeal abscesses) present with neck swelling, stridor, and dysphagia, requiring emergent contrast-enhanced CT, intravenous broad-spectrum antibiotics, and surgical airway evaluation. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tonsillitis, peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for tonsillitis, peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of tonsillitis, peritonsillar abscess and deep neck infection requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42220325**: *Unilateral versus bilateral abscess tonsillectomy. Implications for current surgical practice.* (2026) [review] - The Journal of laryngology and otology

## [1754] Tourette syndrome: tics management, alpha-2 agonists, habit reversal therapy

**Cluster**: tourette syndrome tics management alpha 2 agonists habit reversal therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tourette syndrome: tics management, alpha-2 agonists, habit reversal therapy is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: [Tics and Tourette Syndrome]. (PMID 42659562)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Sleep Disorders in Patients with Tics: Towards Personalized Care for Tourette Syndrome. (PMID 42346620)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Tics, Tourette's and Related Muscle Pain in Children: A Review. (PMID 41718079)
- First-line behavioral therapy for Tourette syndrome is Comprehensive Behavioral Intervention for Tics (CBIT), including habit reversal training, with alpha-2 adrenergic agonists (clonidine, guanfacine) preferred as initial pharmacotherapy over dopamine receptor antagonists due to minimal metabolic and extrapyramidal adverse effects. (PMID 31826075)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tourette syndrome: tics management, alpha-2 agonists, habit reversal therapy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1755] Toxic and nutritional neuropathies: B12, B6 deficiency, chemotherapy-induced

**Cluster**: toxic and nutritional neuropathies b12 b6 deficiency chemotherapy induced | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Toxic and nutritional neuropathies present as insidious, length-dependent, symmetrical sensorimotor polyneuropathies. Recognizing deficiency states (B12, copper) and neurotoxic exposures (pyridoxine excess, platinum agents, taxanes) is essential for arresting axonal degeneration before permanent disability occurs.

### Core Teaching Points
- Nutritional and toxic neuropathies present with symmetric, length-dependent distal sensorimotor polyneuropathy; vitamin B12 (cobalamin) deficiency characteristically produces subacute combined degeneration (SCD) of the spinal cord, combining dorsal column sensory ataxia with lateral corticospinal tract spastic paraparesis. (PMID 42825560)
- While vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) deficiency causes peripheral neuropathy, chronic excessive pyridoxine supplementation (>100-200 mg/day) produces severe, irreversible non-length-dependent sensory neuronopathy (ganglionopathy) affecting dorsal root ganglia with profound sensory ataxia and areflexia. (PMID 42306345)
- Chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy (CIPN) is a major dose-limiting complication of platinum compounds (oxaliplatin acute cold-induced dysesthesias; cisplatin cumulative sensory neuropathy), taxanes (paclitaxel axonal neuropathy), vinca alkaloids (vincristine autonomic and motor neuropathy), and proteasome inhibitors (bortezomib). (PMID 40413439)
- In patients with low-normal serum B12 levels (150-300 pmol/L), elevated functional metabolic intermediates—methylmalonic acid (MMA) and homocysteine—confirm true intracellular cobalamin deficiency; concurrent copper deficiency must be excluded as it closely mimics subacute combined degeneration. (PMID 42825560)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of duloxetine as the only guideline-recommended pharmacotherapy for symptomatic chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy.
- Role of neurofilament light chain (NfL) biomarkers in detecting subclinical early axonal injury before irreversible neuropathy develops.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pyridoxine toxicity paradox: mega-dose vitamin B6 causes pure sensory ganglionopathy with loss of proprioception and preserved motor power.
- Differential diagnosis of subacute combined degeneration: copper deficiency (e.g., from zinc toxicity or gastric bypass) produces identical posterior/lateral column degeneration.
- Oxaliplatin unique toxicity: acute cold-triggered perioral and distal paresthesias occurring during or immediately following infusion.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old strict vegan presenting with progressive unsteady gait, lower limb paresthesias, bilateral extensor plantar responses, and absent vibratory sensation to the costal margins.
- A 42-year-old woman presenting with sensory ataxia and absent tendon reflexes after consuming 300 mg daily of over-the-counter vitamin B6 supplements for 2 years.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42825560**: *Nutritional and Toxic Neuropathies.* (2026) [review] - Continuum (Minneapolis, Minn.)
- **PMID 42306345**: *The Toxic Legacy of Recreational Nitrous Oxide Use: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Multisystem Complications From Functional Vitamin B12 Deficiency.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Cureus
- **PMID 40413439**: *Sensory neuropathy and associated factors among patients living with human immuno-deficiency virus in Africa: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Virology journal

Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points

## [1757] Tracheobronchomalacia

**Cluster**: tracheobronchomalacia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tracheobronchomalacia (TBM) is a pathological condition characterized by dynamic structural weakness and excessive collapsibility of the tracheal and bronchial cartilaginous rings and posterior membranous wall during expiration. It is classified into congenital (primary) forms in infants—often linked to tracheoesophageal fistula or prematurity—and acquired (secondary) forms in adults, triggered by chronic airway inflammation (COPD, relapsing polychondritis), prolonged endotracheal intubation, or trauma. Dynamic flexible bronchoscopy during active forced expiration or coughing is the gold standard for definitive diagnosis, identifying >=50% (and often >=70-80%) dynamic airway lumen collapse.

### Core Teaching Points
- Definitive diagnosis of tracheobronchomalacia requires dynamic flexible bronchoscopy during tidal breathing, deep expiration, and forced coughing, documenting >=50% reduction in luminal cross-sectional area (with severe disease defined as >=70-80% collapse). (PMID 39488358)
- Dynamic multi-detector paired inspiratory-expiratory computed tomography (dynamic CT) serves as the primary non-invasive imaging modality of choice, capturing the 'frown sign' of dynamic crescentic airway narrowing on end-expiratory phases. (PMID 42409109)
- Conservative medical management includes non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (CPAP/BiPAP), which acts as a 'pneumatic stent' to maintain dynamic airway patency during exhalation, paired with aggressive pulmonary clearance and treatment of underlying COPD. (PMID 42679771)
- In severe, highly symptomatic adult TBM refractory to conservative therapy, surgical tracheobronchoplasty (posterior membranous wall stabilization using polypropylene mesh, performed open or robotic-assisted) provides durable symptomatic and functional improvement following a successful temporary stent trial. (PMID 41553634)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term complication rates (granulation tissue, stent migration, mucus plugging) of silicone versus self-expanding metallic stents in adult central airway collapse.
- Efficacy and patient selection for posterior tracheopexy versus aortopexy in paediatric severe tracheomalacia.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic gold standard for TBM: dynamic flexible bronchoscopy during forced expiration or coughing.
- Pneumatic stenting mechanism: explaining how continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) prevents dynamic expiratory collapse.
- Preoperative evaluation for surgical tracheobronchoplasty: utility of a temporary silicone or metallic Y-stent trial to confirm symptomatic reversibility.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old male with severe COPD and recurrent lower respiratory infections presents with a harsh, barking ('seal-like') cough and inability to clear secretions; dynamic expiratory CT reveals 85% collapse of the distal trachea and both mainstem bronchi.
- An 8-month-old infant with a history of repaired esophageal atresia presents with persistent stridor, barking cough, and recurrent reflex apnoeic spells ('dying spells') during feeding.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39488358**: *ACR Appropriateness Criteria® Tracheobronchial Disease.* (2024) [guideline] - Journal of the American College of Radiology : JACR
- **PMID 42409109**: *Posterior tracheopexy for pediatric tracheomalacia: A global evidence synthesis and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of pediatric surgery
- **PMID 42679771**: *Interventions for primary pediatric tracheomalacia: A systematic review of the therapeutic landscape and evidence quality.* (2026) [systematic review] - International journal of pediatric otorhinolaryngology
- **PMID 41553634**: *Robotic-assisted tracheobronchoplasty for excessive central airway collapse: a systematic review of clinical outcomes, safety, and functional recovery.* (2026) [systematic review] - Journal of robotic surgery
- **PMID 42364941**: *Bronchoscopic and interventional management of tracheobronchomalacia in children with bronchopulmonary dysplasia: a review of evidence.* (2026) [review] - Paediatric respiratory reviews

## [1758] Tracheostomy: indications, timing, percutaneous vs surgical technique

**Cluster**: tracheostomy indications timing percutaneous vs surgical technique | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tracheostomy is one of the most common surgical procedures performed in critically ill patients requiring prolonged mechanical ventilation, acute airway obstruction, or chronic pulmonary toilet impairment. Timing of tracheostomy—traditionally categorized into early (within 7-10 days) versus late (>10-14 days)—balances the risks of prolonged translaryngeal intubation (glottic injury, subglottic stenosis) against procedural complications and unnecessary operations in rapidly weaning patients. Percutaneous dilatational tracheostomy (PDT) performed at the bedside in the ICU has largely superseded open surgical tracheostomy (OST) for elective adult patients due to reduced wound infections and comparable safety when guided by real-time bronchoscopy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Major indications for tracheostomy in the ICU include: anticipated prolonged mechanical ventilation, failed extubation, severe neurological deficit with loss of protective airway reflexes, and acute or chronic upper airway obstruction. (PMID 33875932)
- The landmark TracMan trial demonstrated that early tracheostomy (within day 4) does not improve 30-day or 1-year mortality compared to late tracheostomy (after day 10), but significantly reduces the duration of sedation and ICU length of stay. (PMID 41040762)
- Percutaneous dilatational tracheostomy (PDT) at the bedside is associated with lower surgical site infection rates, smaller incisions, less tissue trauma, and reduced hospital costs compared to open surgical tracheostomy, provided anatomical contraindications are absent. (PMID 41845239)
- Real-time bronchoscopic visualization during percutaneous dilatational tracheostomy significantly reduces catastrophic procedural complications, including posterior tracheal wall laceration, paratracheal false-passage placement, and pneumothorax. (PMID 41273066)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of tracheostomy in acute cervical spinal cord injury (day 3-5 vs later) to prevent pulmonary complications and facilitate diaphragm weaning.
- Standardized protocols for decannulation readiness using peak cough flow, blue dye testing, and capping trials in ICU survivors.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- TracMan trial findings: early vs late tracheostomy impact on mortality (no difference) vs sedation requirements and ICU stay (improved with early).
- Anatomical contraindications to bedside percutaneous tracheostomy favoring open surgical tracheostomy (morbid obesity with unidentifiable landmarks, gross thyroid goitre, cervical spine instability, severe coagulopathy).
- Role of continuous bronchoscopic guidance during percutaneous dilational tracheostomy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old male with severe traumatic brain injury remains comatose (GCS 6) on mechanical ventilation on day 8; the ICU team plans elective tracheostomy for airway protection and weaning.
- During a bedside percutaneous dilatational tracheostomy, the intensivist utilizes dynamic bronchoscopy to guide needle puncture between the second and third tracheal cartilages.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33875932**: *Update on Pediatric Tracheostomy: Indications, Technique, Education, and Decannulation.* (2021) [review] - Current otorhinolaryngology reports
- **PMID 41040762**: *Surgical Management of Infective Endocarditis: Indications, Techniques, and Outcomes.* (2025) [literature] - None
- **PMID 41845239**: *Percutaneous dilatational tracheostomy with versus without bronchoscopic guidance: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - BMC anesthesiology
- **PMID 41273066**: *Tracheostomy in Flap-Based Head and Neck Cancer Surgery: A Meta-Analysis of Indications and Adverse Outcomes.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Head & neck
- **PMID 42763627**: *Mediastinal tracheostomy: techniques, outcomes and quality of life-a systematic review.* (2026) [systematic review] - Mediastinum (Hong Kong, China)

## [1759] Tranexamic acid and damage control resuscitation in trauma

**Cluster**: tranexamic acid and damage control resuscitation in trauma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Damage control resuscitation (DCR) is the modern paradigm for resuscitating severe trauma patients with life-threatening haemorrhagic shock, targeting the lethal triad of coagulopathy, hypothermia, and acidosis. DCR integrates permissive hypotension (MAP ~50-65 mmHg pending surgical haemostasis), balanced blood component transfusion ratios (1:1:1 of packed RBCs, fresh frozen plasma, and platelets), and early antifibrinolytic therapy. Tranexamic acid (TXA)—a synthetic lysine analogue that competitively blocks plasminogen activation—was proven by the CRASH-2 trial to reduce trauma mortality when administered within 3 hours of injury, while administration after 3 hours increases mortality.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark CRASH-2 trial demonstrated that early administration of tranexamic acid (1 g IV bolus over 10 minutes followed by 1 g IV infusion over 8 hours) significantly reduces all-cause mortality and death due to bleeding in trauma patients with significant haemorrhage. (PMID 41453514)
- Tranexamic acid must be administered within 3 hours of injury; administration after 3 hours is associated with increased bleeding-related mortality and is strictly contraindicated. (PMID 41446790)
- Damage control resuscitation prioritizes balanced 1:1:1 transfusion ratios of packed red blood cells, plasma, and platelets, minimizing large-volume crystalloid infusions to prevent dilutional coagulopathy and endothelial glycocalyx disruption (PROPPR trial). (PMID 41423257)
- In patients with traumatic brain injury (TBI), the CRASH-3 trial demonstrated that TXA reduces head injury-related death in mild-to-moderate TBI (GCS 9-15) when given within 3 hours of trauma, with negligible benefit in severe TBI (GCS 3-8). (PMID 42711637)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and vascular thromboembolism risks of pre-hospital TXA administration versus in-hospital administration (PATCH-Trauma trial findings).
- Individualized viscoelastometric testing (TEG / ROTEM) guided TXA dosing versus standardized empiric 2 g bolus protocols.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CRASH-2 timing rule: explaining why TXA must be given within 3 hours of trauma injury and why post-3-hour administration increases mortality.
- Dosing regimen of TXA in trauma resuscitation: 1 g IV loading dose over 10 minutes, followed by 1 g IV infusion over 8 hours.
- Damage control resuscitation components: permissive hypotension, 1:1:1 transfusion ratio, and limitation of crystalloids.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old male sustains multiple gunshot wounds to the abdomen, presenting 45 minutes post-injury with BP 74/42 mmHg, HR 135 bpm, and massive external haemorrhage; the trauma team initiates the massive transfusion protocol and TXA bolus.
- A motorcyclist presents 4 hours after a severe highway collision with pelvic fractures and bleeding; the junior trainee asks whether IV tranexamic acid should still be infused.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41453514**: *Perioperative tranexamic acid in burn surgery: systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Brazilian journal of anesthesiology (Elsevier)
- **PMID 41446790**: *Is tranexamic acid effective for all traumatic brain injury patients? a severity based systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in pharmacology
- **PMID 41423257**: *Explosive Weapons Trauma Care Collective (EXTRACCT) Clinical Practice Guideline: Resuscitation of Pediatric Blast Injury Patient.* (2026) [guideline] - World journal of surgery
- **PMID 42711637**: *Tranexamic Acid Administration in Laparoscopic Sleeve Gastrectomy.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Obesity surgery
- **PMID 42739888**: *Efficacy and Safety of Tranexamic Acid in Abdominoplasty: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of clinical medicine

## [1762] Transcatheter aortic valve implantation (TAVI): patient selection, access, complications

**Cluster**: transcatheter aortic valve implantation tavi patient selection access complications | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Transcatheter aortic valve implantation (TAVI, or TAVR) has revolutionized the treatment of severe symptomatic native aortic stenosis across prohibitive, high, intermediate, and low surgical risk patients. Multidisciplinary 'Heart Team' evaluation integrates anatomical suitability on ECG-gated multidetector CT (annulus dimensions, coronary ostial heights, calcification distribution, and transfemoral access caliber) with clinical risk scores (STS, EuroSCORE II) and patient life expectancy. Although transfemoral TAVI achieves outstanding hemodynamics and rapid recovery, operators must anticipate and manage specific complications: high-degree atrioventricular conduction block requiring permanent pacemaker, paravalvular regurgitation, vascular access injury, and stroke.

### Core Teaching Points
- Transfemoral access is the preferred first-line vascular approach for TAVI (>90% of cases), requiring minimum iliofemoral luminal diameters (typically >=5.0-5.5 mm depending on sheath size) without circumferential calcification or severe tortuosity. (PMID 42622107)
- Pre-procedural ECG-gated multislice cardiac CT is mandatory to measure the aortic valve annulus area/perimeter, evaluate coronary artery take-off heights (distance <10-12 mm from annulus confers risk of acute coronary ostial obstruction), and plan coplanar fluoroscopic angles. (PMID 42808440)
- New-onset high-degree atrioventricular block and permanent pacemaker (PPM) implantation remain frequent complications, occurring more often with self-expanding valves (15-25%) than balloon-expandable valves (5-10%), due to mechanical compression of the membranous septum and His bundle. (PMID 42876001)
- Paravalvular aortic regurgitation (PVL)—arising from incomplete frame sealing in heavily calcified commissures—is associated with increased long-term mortality and is classified by VARC-3 criteria using Doppler echocardiography. (PMID 42846256)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term structural valve deterioration (SVD) and durability of transcatheter bioprostheses beyond 10 years compared to surgical bioprosthetic valves.
- Optimal antithrombotic therapy post-TAVI in patients without baseline anticoagulation indications (single antiplatelet therapy [SAPT] with aspirin vs DAPT vs oral anticoagulants).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Identifying risk factors for acute coronary ostial obstruction during TAVI (coronary height <10-12 mm and shallow sinuses of Valsalva).
- Predictors and rates of permanent pacemaker requirement comparing self-expanding vs balloon-expandable prostheses.
- Patient selection: transcatheter versus surgical aortic valve replacement (SAVR) in younger low-risk patients (<65-70 years) where valve durability, bicuspid anatomy, and lifetime coronary access are primary concerns.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 82-year-old female with severe symptomatic aortic stenosis (mean gradient 48 mmHg, valve area 0.6 cm2) and STS score 6.2% is evaluated for transfemoral TAVI; CT shows coronary ostia at 14 mm and femoral arteries measuring 6.5 mm.
- Following successful deployment of a self-expanding transcatheter aortic valve, a patient develops new right bundle branch block with first-degree AV block and intermittent 2:1 Mobitz II block on telemetry.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42622107**: *Redo-Transcatheter Aortic Valve Implantation Versus Surgical Explantation in Patients With Previous Transcatheter Aortic Valve Implantation: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of the American Heart Association
- **PMID 42808440**: *Incidence and Predictors of Complications After Transcatheter Aortic Valve Implantation According to VARC-3 Criteria: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of the American Heart Association
- **PMID 42876001**: *Short-Term Complications of Transcatheter Aortic Valve Replacement: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (PROLONG-TAVR).* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Catheterization and cardiovascular interventions : official journal of the Society for Cardiac Angiography & Interventions

## [1764] Transfusion-Associated Circulatory Overload

**Cluster**: transfusion associated circulatory overload | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Transfusion-Associated Circulatory Overload (TACO) is an acute, hydrostatic pulmonary oedema precipitated by the transfusion of blood products, and represents the leading cause of transfusion-related morbidity and mortality in high-income countries. Pathophysiology involves excessive volume, rapid infusion rate, or impaired cardiovascular/renal compensation, leading to elevated left atrial pressures and fluid extravasation into lung alveoli. Differentiating TACO from Transfusion-Related Acute Lung Injury (TRALI)—which is non-cardiogenic and mediated by neutrophil activation—is critical, as TACO responds dramatically to diuresis and positive pressure ventilation whereas TRALI does not.

### Core Teaching Points
- TACO is defined clinically by acute respiratory distress developing within 6 (or up to 12) hours of blood product transfusion, with evidence of hydrostatic cardiac overload (tachycardia, hypertension, elevated JVP, peripheral oedema, cardiomegaly/pulmonary oedema on CXR, and marked elevation in BNP or NT-proBNP). (PMID 40342068)
- Major risk factors for TACO include advanced age (>70 years), pre-existing congestive heart failure, chronic kidney disease, hypoalbuminaemia, rapid infusion rates, and large-volume transfusion. (PMID 35285900)
- TACO is distinguished from TRALI by the presence of systemic hypertension (versus hypotension in TRALI), elevated jugular venous pressure, responsive diuresis with loop diuretics, and post-transfusion BNP elevation >1.5-fold baseline. (PMID 42561330)
- Prevention requires pre-transfusion risk assessment, restrictive single-unit red cell transfusion strategies, slowing infusion rates (transfusing a single unit over 3 to 4 hours), and prophylactic pre- or mid-transfusion loop diuretics (e.g., IV furosemide 20-40 mg) in high-risk patients. (PMID 42074640)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic accuracy and specific cutoff thresholds for pre- versus post-transfusion NT-proBNP in differentiating mixed TACO/TRALI presentations.
- Effectiveness of automated electronic health record clinical decision support alerts in prompting prophylactic diuretic orders for high-risk patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating TACO from TRALI: hypertension, elevated JVP, high post-transfusion BNP/NT-proBNP, and rapid response to IV furosemide definitively favor TACO.
- Immediate emergency management of suspected TACO: stop transfusion, sit patient upright, administer high-flow oxygen/CPAP, and give intravenous loop diuretics.
- Prophylactic strategies for TACO in high-risk patients (pre-transfusion IV furosemide, single-unit transfusion over 3-4 hours).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 78-year-old woman with ischemic cardiomyopathy and CKD stage 3 receives 2 units of packed RBCs over 2 hours for anaemia; 45 minutes after the second unit, she develops acute dyspnoea, BP rises to 185/100 mmHg, and JVP is 8 cm.
- A hospital transfusion committee reviews an adverse reaction where an elderly oncology patient developed pulmonary infiltrates after plasma infusion; the team analyzes post-transfusion BNP and hemodynamic parameters.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 35285900**: *Oncology Patients Who Develop Transfusion-Associated Circulatory Overload: An Observational Study.* (2022) [review] - Laboratory medicine

## [1766] Transoesophageal echocardiography: indications, LA thrombus, valve assessment

**Cluster**: transoesophageal echocardiography indications la thrombus valve assessment | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Transoesophageal echocardiography (TOE/TEE) provides high-resolution ultrasonic visualization of posterior cardiac structures unobstructed by chest wall, ribs, or lung parenchyma. Because the transducer sits in the mid-oesophagus directly posterior to the left atrium, TOE is the undisputed gold standard for detecting left atrial appendage (LAA) thrombi, ruling out infective endocarditis vegetations and perivalvular abscesses, and guiding transcatheter structural heart interventions. Safe performance demands pre-procedure evaluation of oesophageal pathology contraindications, conscious sedation monitoring, and multiplane probe manipulation.

### Core Teaching Points
- TOE is the gold standard modality for excluding left atrial appendage (LAA) thrombus prior to direct current cardioversion or catheter ablation for atrial fibrillation/flutter, exhibiting sensitivity and specificity approaching 99-100%. (PMID 38901557)
- In suspected infective endocarditis (IE), TOE is strongly indicated when transthoracic echo (TTE) is negative or non-diagnostic despite high clinical suspicion, in all patients with prosthetic valves or intracardiac devices, and to detect invasive perivalvular complications (root abscess, fistula, leaflet perforation). (PMID 42603603)
- Comprehensive prosthetic valve assessment by TOE evaluates mechanism of dysfunction (structural leaflet degeneration, pannus ingrowth vs mobile thrombus, paravalvular regurgitant jets, rocking of the sewing ring in dehiscence). (PMID 41613485)
- Absolute contraindications to TOE include oesophageal pathology with risk of perforation: oesophageal stricture, oesophageal carcinoma, active upper gastrointestinal bleeding, oesophageal perforation, Zenker diverticulum, and recent oesophageal surgery. (PMID 39099755)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic equivalence of modern 4D cardiac CT angiography compared to TOE for pre-procedural left atrial appendage thrombus exclusion.
- Routine utility of TOE versus surface POCUS during active cardiopulmonary resuscitation in out-of-hospital cardiac arrest.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mandatory TOE indications in infective endocarditis: prosthetic valves, intracardiac electronic devices, and suspected perivalvular abscess.
- Excluding LAA thrombus before cardioversion: identifying 'smoke' (spontaneous echo contrast, a precursor of stasis) versus discrete mobile thrombus in the pectinate muscles.
- Identifying absolute contraindications to TOE probe insertion (active GI bleeding, oesophageal malignancy/stricture, Zenker diverticulum).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old male with persistent atrial fibrillation of unknown duration is scheduled for electrical cardioversion; TOE is performed, demonstrating a 1.2 cm hyperechoic mobile mass in the left atrial appendage apex.
- A 55-year-old female with a mechanical aortic valve presents with persistent Enterococcus faecalis bacteraemia and new-onset PR-interval prolongation; TTE is inconclusive, prompting urgent TOE.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38901557**: *Transoesophageal echocardiography beyond the echo-laboratory. An expert consensus paper of the working group of echocardiography of the hellenic society of cardiology.* (2024) [guideline] - Hellenic journal of cardiology : HJC = Hellenike kardiologike epitheorese
- **PMID 42603603**: *Transoesophageal echocardiography during cardiopulmonary resuscitation for cardiac arrest: a scoping review.* (2026) [review] - Resuscitation
- **PMID 41613485**: *Anaesthesia and perioperative transoesophageal echocardiography in obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.* (2026) [review] - Indian journal of thoracic and cardiovascular surgery

## [1767] Transthyretin amyloidosis treatment

**Cluster**: transthyretin amyloidosis treatment | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Transthyretin amyloidosis (ATTR) is a progressive, fatal systemic protein-misfolding disorder caused by the dissociation of transthyretin tetramers into misfolded monomers that aggregate as insoluble amyloid fibrils in myocardial and neural tissues. ATTR manifests as ATTR cardiomyopathy (ATTR-CM) and/or ATTR polyneuropathy (ATTR-PN), occurring in both wild-type (ATTRwt, predominantly in older men) and hereditary variant (ATTRv) forms. The therapeutic landscape has undergone a revolutionary shift from purely supportive care to targeted disease-modifying therapies: kinetic tetramer stabilizers (tafamidis, acoramidis) and hepatic gene-silencing therapies (RNA interference: patisiran, vutrisiran; antisense oligonucleotides: inotersen, eplontersen).

### Core Teaching Points
- Tafamidis is a kinetic stabilizer that binds with high affinity to the thyroxine-binding sites of the TTR tetramer, preventing dissociation into amyloidogenic monomers; the landmark ATTR-ACT trial established that tafamidis reduces all-cause mortality by 30% and cardiovascular hospitalizations in ATTR cardiomyopathy. (PMID 42265773)
- Non-invasive diagnostic criteria for ATTR cardiomyopathy allow diagnosis without endomyocardial biopsy if patients have typical heart failure/echo findings, Grade 2 or 3 cardiac uptake on 99mTc-pyrophosphate (PYP) or DPND bone scintigraphy, AND completely negative serum/urine monoclonal protein studies (immunofixation and free light chains). (PMID 42544292)
- RNA interference (RNAi) therapeutics—specifically patisiran (IV every 3 weeks) and vutrisiran (subcutaneous every 3 months)—target hepatic TTR mRNA, degrading the transcript and reducing serum transthyretin production by >80-85%, halting or reversing polyneuropathy progression (APOLLO and HELIOS-A trials). (PMID 42549565)
- The HELIOS-B trial demonstrated that vutrisiran significantly reduces all-cause mortality and recurrent cardiovascular events in patients with ATTR cardiomyopathy, establishing subcutaneous gene silencers as potent disease-modifying agents in cardiac disease. (PMID 42572428)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term combination therapy: whether combining a TTR gene silencer (vutrisiran) with a tetramer stabilizer (tafamidis) provides additive clinical benefit over monotherapy.
- Role of emerging in vivo CRISPR-Cas9 gene editing (e.g., nexiguran ziclumeran) in achieving permanent single-dose suppression of mutant and wild-type TTR.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ATTR-ACT trial landmark findings: tafamidis reduces 30-month mortality and cardiovascular hospitalizations in ATTR-CM.
- Non-invasive diagnosis requirements: positive 99mTc-PYP/DPD scan (grade 2-3) PLUS strict exclusion of AL amyloidosis via serum and urine immunofixation and serum free light chains.
- Mechanism of action of gene silencers (vutrisiran, patisiran: mRNA degradation) versus stabilizers (tafamidis, acoramidis: tetramer stabilization).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 76-year-old male presents with HFpEF, bilateral carpal tunnel syndrome, and low-voltage QRS on ECG despite marked concentric LV wall thickening (16 mm); 99mTc-PYP scan shows intense Grade 3 myocardial uptake with normal light chains.
- A 54-year-old woman with confirmed hereditary ATTR (Val30Met mutation) presents with progressive sensorimotor peripheral neuropathy and orthostatic hypotension, evaluated for subcutaneous vutrisiran initiation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42265773**: *Mortality in octogenarian patients with transthyretin amyloidosis treated with tafamidis: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Orphanet journal of rare diseases
- **PMID 42544292**: *Clinical outcomes with vutrisiran in transthyretin amyloidosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Global cardiology science & practice
- **PMID 42549565**: *A Network Meta-Analysis of Hereditary Transthyretin Amyloidosis With Polyneuropathy Supports a Class Effect, Not a Ranking of Individual Drugs.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - European journal of neurology
- **PMID 42572428**: *Reply to: "A Network Meta-Analysis of Hereditary Transthyretin Amyloidosis With Polyneuropathy Supports a Class Effect, Not a Ranking of Individual Drugs".* (2026) [meta-analysis] - European journal of neurology

## [1770] Trauma resuscitation and head injury

**Cluster**: trauma resuscitation and head injury | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The coexistence of severe traumatic brain injury (TBI) and systemic haemorrhagic shock represents a formidable clinical resuscitation dilemma. While damage control resuscitation for systemic trauma advocates permissive hypotension (systolic BP ~80-90 mmHg) to limit clot disruption, systemic hypotension in severe TBI doubles mortality by precipitating secondary cerebral ischemic injury. Resuscitation must aggressively prioritize cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) maintenance—strictly avoiding systolic BP <100-110 mmHg—alongside rapid haemostasis, normoxia, normocarbia, and timely surgical intracranial decompression.

### Core Teaching Points
- In patients with combined severe TBI and systemic haemorrhagic trauma, 'permissive hypotension' is strictly contraindicated; the Brain Trauma Foundation guidelines mandate maintaining systolic BP >=100 mmHg for patients aged 50-69 years and >=110 mmHg for patients aged 15-49 or >70 years to prevent secondary ischemic brain injury. (PMID 41423257)
- A single documented episode of systemic hypotension (SBP <90 mmHg) or hypoxemia (PaO2 <60 mmHg / SpO2 <90%) in severe TBI is independently associated with a doubling of mortality. (PMID 42423140)
- Damage control fluid resuscitation in concomitant TBI requires balanced blood components (1:1:1 pRBC, FFP, platelets) or whole blood; hypotonic crystalloids (such as 0.9% normal saline with low osmolarity relative to injured brain, and especially Ringer's lactate with osmolarity ~273 mOsm/L) must be avoided to prevent worsening cerebral oedema. (PMID 36272729)
- Tranexamic acid (TXA) should be administered within 3 hours of injury in polytrauma patients with significant bleeding; in head injury (CRASH-3 trial), TXA reduces mortality in mild-to-moderate TBI without expanding intracranial hematomas. (PMID 27654000)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal blood pressure targets guided by non-invasive cerebral autoregulation monitoring (PRx) versus standardized SBP cutoffs in multi-trauma TBI.
- Use of pre-hospital whole blood transfusion versus component therapy in polytrauma patients with blunt intracranial hemorrhage.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Modifying permissive hypotension targets when severe traumatic brain injury coexists with torso haemorrhage (maintaining SBP >=100-110 mmHg).
- Why hypotonic fluids (like Ringer's lactate) are avoided in severe TBI (hypotonicity increases brain tissue water content and elevates ICP).
- Impact of secondary physiological insults (hypotension, hypoxia, hyperthermia, hypocapnia) on TBI mortality.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old unrestrained driver sustains blunt abdominal trauma with pelvic fracture and severe closed head injury (GCS 6); BP is 82/46 mmHg, and the trauma team must choose blood pressure targets and resuscitation fluids.
- An intubated TBI patient in the trauma bay has a sudden drop in PaCO2 to 24 mmHg due to accidental hyperventilation on the transport ventilator, causing cerebral vasoconstriction.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41423257**: *Explosive Weapons Trauma Care Collective (EXTRACCT) Clinical Practice Guideline: Resuscitation of Pediatric Blast Injury Patient.* (2026) [guideline] - World journal of surgery
- **PMID 42423140**: *Explosive Weapons Trauma Care Collective (EXTRACCT) Clinical Practice Guideline: Transfer of Critically Injured Casualties.* (2026) [guideline] - World journal of surgery

## [1771] Traumatic brain injury ICU management

**Cluster**: traumatic brain injury icu management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intensive care management of severe traumatic brain injury (TBI, defined as post-resuscitation GCS <=8) focuses on the prevention, prompt detection, and aggressive mitigation of secondary brain injury. Secondary insults—ischemia, cellular energy failure, cerebral oedema, intracranial hypertension, and non-convulsive seizures—occur hours to days after the primary mechanical injury. A structured tier-based protocol coordinates mechanical ventilation, hemodynamic optimization targeting cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP 60-70 mmHg), automated pupillometry, temperature control, and multi-modality neuromonitoring.

### Core Teaching Points
- Continuous intracranial pressure (ICP) monitoring is recommended in severe TBI patients (GCS <=8) with an abnormal admission head CT (hematomas, contusions, swelling, compressed basal cisterns) or normal CT with >=2 clinical risk factors (age >40, motor posturing, SBP <90 mmHg). (PMID 27654000)
- Guideline-directed management targets an ICP threshold <22 mmHg and a cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP = MAP - ICP) between 60 and 70 mmHg; avoiding CPP <60 mmHg prevents cerebral ischemia, while avoiding CPP >70 mmHg prevents hyperperfusion and ARDS. (PMID 42768896)
- Prophylactic post-traumatic seizure prevention with levetiracetam or phenytoin is recommended for the first 7 days following severe TBI to decrease early post-traumatic seizures; routine extension beyond 7 days is not recommended as it does not prevent late epilepsy. (PMID 42575227)
- Automated quantitative pupillometry (Neurological Pupil Index [NPI]) provides objective, operator-independent measurement of pupillary reactivity, detecting impending brain herniation and elevated ICP hours before gross clinical changes. (PMID 42547613)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of advanced multi-modality neuromonitoring (brain tissue oxygen tension [PbtO2] and microdialysis) in improving neurological recovery (BOOST-3 trial).
- Optimal timing and patient selection for secondary decompressive craniectomy in medically refractory intracranial hypertension (RESCUEicp trial).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Therapeutic target ranges for ICP (<22 mmHg) and CPP (60-70 mmHg) per Brain Trauma Foundation guidelines.
- Duration and drug selection for post-traumatic seizure prophylaxis (7 days of levetiracetam or phenytoin; lack of benefit beyond 7 days).
- Avoiding aggressive routine hyperventilation (PaCO2 <30-35 mmHg) due to profound cerebral vasoconstriction and secondary ischemic necrosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 30-year-old male with severe TBI post-evacuation of an acute subdural hematoma has an external ventricular drain placed; monitor displays ICP 26 mmHg and MAP 82 mmHg (CPP = 56 mmHg).
- A patient with severe TBI on day 3 in the neuro-ICU demonstrates a decline in automated pupillometry NPI from 4.2 to 2.1 in the left eye despite stable sedation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27654000**: *Guidelines for the Management of Severe Traumatic Brain Injury, Fourth Edition.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 42768896**: *Sleep-Wake Disturbances in Pediatric Traumatic Brain Injury: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Interventions.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Brain and behavior
- **PMID 42575227**: *Effectiveness of hyperbaric oxygen in traumatic brain injury patients: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Complementary therapies in medicine
- **PMID 42547613**: *Personality change after traumatic brain injury: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of neurology
- **PMID 42477220**: *Prognostic value of automated pupillometry in traumatic brain injury: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Neurosurgical review

## [1772] Traumatic brain injury intracranial pressure management

**Cluster**: traumatic brain injury intracranial pressure management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Elevated intracranial pressure (ICP >22 mmHg) following severe traumatic brain injury reflects the Monro-Kellie doctrine: within the rigid cranium, expanding volume (hematoma, brain oedema, vascular engorgement) compromises compensatory reserve, resulting in brain tissue herniation and death. Management utilizes a structured, tiered protocol escalating from basic neuroprotective foundations to advanced surgical and medical interventions. Tier 1 interventions include head elevation (30 degrees), midline neck positioning, analgesia/sedation, and CSF drainage; Tier 2 incorporates hyperosmolar therapy (mannitol or hypertonic saline); Tier 3 reserves decompressive craniectomy, barbiturate coma, or therapeutic hypothermia for refractory crises.

### Core Teaching Points
- Tier 1 baseline neuroprotective measures include: elevating the head of the bed to 30 degrees to optimize jugular venous outflow, maintaining the cervical spine in neutral midline position, ensuring normothermia (36-37 C), light-to-moderate sedation, and intermittent CSF drainage via external ventricular drain (EVD). (PMID 36272729)
- Tier 2 hyperosmolar therapy utilizes either equimolar boluses of mannitol (0.25 to 1.0 g/kg) or hypertonic saline (3% NaCl 150-250 mL bolus, or 23.4% NaCl 30 mL bolus); hypertonic saline is preferred in hemodynamically unstable or hypotensive patients as it expands plasma volume without inducing osmotic diuresis. (PMID 27654000)
- Mild hypocapnia (PaCO2 target 30-35 mmHg) may be utilized temporarily as a temporizing bridge for acute intracranial hypertension crises, but prolonged or severe hyperventilation (PaCO2 <30 mmHg) causes severe cerebral vasoconstriction and tissue ischemia. (PMID 42768896)
- Tier 3 therapies for refractory intracranial hypertension include surgical secondary decompressive craniectomy (RESCUEicp trial), high-dose continuous barbiturate infusion (pentobarbital or thiopental titrated to burst suppression), and deep neuromuscular blockade. (PMID 42575227)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative long-term neurological functional outcomes between 3% hypertonic saline continuous infusions versus intermittent bolus administration.
- Safety and efficacy of ultra-early secondary decompressive craniectomy versus medical refractory rescue in diffuse brain swelling.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Choosing between mannitol and hypertonic saline: why hypertonic saline is preferred in hypotensive trauma patients (preserves intravascular volume vs osmotic dehydration).
- Understanding the dangers of prolonged hyperventilation in ICP management (cerebral vasoconstriction leading to brain tissue hypoxia).
- Stepwise tier escalation for intracranial hypertension refractory to Tier 1 and Tier 2 measures.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An intubated 22-year-old male with severe diffuse axonal injury develops sustained ICP of 28 mmHg for 20 minutes despite EVD drainage and sedation; serum sodium is 138 mmol/L and BP is 105/65 mmHg.
- A neuro-ICU patient with refractory ICP >30 mmHg fails hyperosmolar boluses; the neurosurgical team is consulted to discuss decompressive craniectomy versus pentobarbital infusion.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1774] Treatment of psoriatic arthritis: NSAIDs, csDMARDs, IL-17, IL-23, TNF inhibitors

**Cluster**: treatment of psoriatic arthritis nsaids csdmards il 17 il 23 tnf inhibitors | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Psoriatic arthritis (PsA) is a chronic, heterogeneous inflammatory spondyloarthritis affecting peripheral joints, axial spine, entheses (enthesitis), and digits (dactylitis), intimately associated with cutaneous psoriasis and nail dystrophy. Management follows domain-based treatment recommendations (GRAPPA and EULAR guidelines), selecting therapies according to active disease domains (peripheral arthritis, axial disease, enthesitis, dactylitis, skin, nails). Treatment escalates from NSAIDs and conventional synthetic DMARDs (methotrexate) to targeted biological DMARDs—TNF inhibitors, IL-17 inhibitors (secukinumab, ixekizumab, bimekizumab), and IL-23 inhibitors (guselkumab, risankizumab)—and targeted synthetic JAK inhibitors.

### Core Teaching Points
- Treatment selection in psoriatic arthritis is domain-driven: while conventional synthetic DMARDs (methotrexate, leflunomide, sulfasalazine) are effective for peripheral arthritis, they are completely INEFFECTIVE for axial disease and enthesitis. (PMID 42373464)
- Tumour necrosis factor (TNF) inhibitors (adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, certolizumab, golimumab) demonstrate robust efficacy across all clinical domains of PsA, including peripheral arthritis, axial disease, enthesitis, dactylitis, and radiographic structural progression. (PMID 42629706)
- Interleukin-17 (IL-17A and IL-17F) inhibitors (secukinumab, ixekizumab, bimekizumab) and IL-23 inhibitors (guselkumab, risankizumab) provide superior cutaneous clearance (PASI 90/100) compared to TNF inhibitors, alongside equivalent peripheral articular efficacy. (PMID 42130150)
- In patients with prominent axial involvement or severe enthesitis refractory to NSAIDs, biologic therapy with a TNF inhibitor or IL-17 inhibitor should be initiated directly, bypassing ineffective conventional synthetic DMARDs. (PMID 42579249)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term disease-modifying potential of early biologic therapy in preventing the transition from cutaneous psoriasis to overt inflammatory psoriatic arthritis.
- Comparative persistence and anti-drug antibody formation between fully humanized monoclonal antibodies and soluble receptor fusion proteins.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Domain-specific efficacy: recognizing that methotrexate does not treat axial PsA or enthesitis.
- Selecting IL-17 or IL-23 inhibitors over TNF inhibitors in patients with severe, extensive plaque psoriasis or concurrent inflammatory bowel disease considerations (IL-17 inhibitors are contraindicated in active Crohn disease).
- Monitoring and safety screening before starting biologic DMARDs (latent tuberculosis screening, hepatitis B/C serology).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old male with severe plaque psoriasis presents with severe heel pain (Achilles enthesitis) and inflammatory back pain; spinal MRI confirms bilateral sacroiliitis; the rheumatologist discusses why methotrexate should be bypassed.
- A 45-year-old woman with active polyarticular psoriatic arthritis and extensive cutaneous psoriasis fails methotrexate 20 mg weekly; the team evaluates starting an IL-17 inhibitor versus TNF inhibitor.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42629706**: *Efficacy and safety evaluation of adalimumab for psoriatic arthritis: A meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Medicine
- **PMID 42130150**: *Efficacy, Safety, and Tolerability of Secukinumab in Psoriatic Arthritis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The Annals of pharmacotherapy

## [1775] Treatment-resistant depression ketamine and ECT

**Cluster**: treatment resistant depression ketamine and ect | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Treatment-resistant depression (TRD)—defined clinically as the failure to achieve symptomatic remission despite adequate trials (dose and duration >=6-8 weeks) of at least two antidepressant medications from different pharmacological classes. TRD is associated with marked psychosocial impairment, increased healthcare utilization, and high suicide risk. When standard pharmacotherapies and oral augmentation (atypical antipsychotics, lithium) fail, advanced somatic and rapid-acting neuroplastic interventions are indicated: electroconvulsive therapy (ECT, the historical gold standard for efficacy) and glutamatergic modulators (intranasal esketamine and intravenous ketamine).

### Core Teaching Points
- Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) remains the single most acutely effective intervention for severe treatment-resistant and psychotic depression, achieving response rates of 70-80% and rapid resolution of acute suicidal intent. (PMID 42863798)
- Right unilateral ultrabrief pulse ECT delivers clinical antidepressant efficacy comparable to bilateral ECT while dramatically reducing retrograde and anterograde cognitive and memory adverse effects. (PMID 42100880)
- Intravenous ketamine (0.5 mg/kg infused over 40 minutes) and intranasal esketamine (S-enantiomer, administered with oral antidepressant) produce rapid, robust antidepressant and anti-suicidal effects within 2 to 4 hours by blocking NMDA receptors and triggering AMPA-mediated BDNF synaptic neuroplasticity. (PMID 42611840)
- Maintenance strategies are mandatory following acute response to both ECT and ketamine/esketamine, as relapse rates exceed 50-60% within 1 to 3 months without continuation maintenance ECT (c-ECT) or ongoing scheduled esketamine dosing. (PMID 42155204)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative long-term cognitive outcomes and sustained durability between maintenance ECT and maintenance esketamine protocols in TRD.
- Risk of dependency, bladder cystitis, and neurocognitive decline with repeated, multi-year ketamine infusions.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Indications for urgent ECT: psychotic depression, severe catatonia, acute life-threatening suicidality, or severe malnutrition from depressive stupor.
- Electrode placement and pulse width tradeoffs in ECT (bilateral vs right unilateral; ultrabrief pulse reducing cognitive/memory side effects).
- Mechanism of action and administrative monitoring for intranasal esketamine (mandatory 2-hour clinical observation for sedation and blood pressure surges).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old woman with severe recurrent MDD fails sertraline, venlafaxine, and aripiprazole augmentation, presenting with profound depressive stupor and food refusal; urgent ECT is planned.
- A 36-year-old male with severe TRD and active suicidal ideation receives an infusion of IV ketamine (0.5 mg/kg), experiencing substantial symptom relief and resolution of suicidal urges within 4 hours.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42863798**: *Augmentation strategies for intravenous ketamine in treatment-resistant depression: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of affective disorders
- **PMID 42100880**: *Cognitive effects of intravenous Ketamine in treatment-resistant depression: A systematic review.* (2026) [systematic review] - Psychological medicine
- **PMID 42611840**: *A Systematic Review of Long-Term Safety Outcomes of Ketamine and Esketamine in Patients with Treatment-Resistant Depression.* (2026) [systematic review] - Psychotherapy and psychosomatics
- **PMID 42155204**: *Short-term effects of oral ketamine for the treatment of depression: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Asian journal of psychiatry
- **PMID 41616859**: *Systematic review and meta-analysis of intranasal esketamine for treatment-resistant depression: Evidence from real-world studies.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of affective disorders

## [1776] Tricuspid regurgitation: functional vs primary, transcatheter repair options

**Cluster**: tricuspid regurgitation functional vs primary transcatheter repair options | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tricuspid regurgitation (TR) is etiologically divided into secondary (functional, >90% of cases) and primary (organic, <10%) valvular heart disease. Secondary TR is driven by annular dilation and leaflet tethering secondary to left-sided heart disease / pulmonary hypertension (ventricular functional TR) or atrial fibrillation with right atrial enlargement (atrial functional TR). Primary TR results from direct structural damage (endocarditis, carcinoid syndrome, rheumatic disease, congenital Ebstein anomaly, or pacemaker lead-induced leaflet perforation). Accurate differentiation dictates medical versus surgical or transcatheter intervention (TEER, transcatheter annuloplasty, heterotopic caval valve implantation, or transcatheter valve replacement).

### Core Teaching Points
- Functional (secondary) TR is characterized by normal tricuspid valve leaflet morphology with pathological annular dilation (>40 mm or >21 mm/m2) and leaflet tethering driven by RV pressure/volume overload or chronic atrial fibrillation (atrial functional TR). (PMID 41984487)
- Primary TR features intrinsic structural leaflet abnormalities—leaflet prolapse/flail, rheumatic retraction, carcinoid plaque thickening, infective endocarditis vegetations, or direct transvalvular pacemaker lead impingement/laceration. (PMID 40810290)
- Surgical tricuspid repair with a prosthetic annuloplasty ring is strongly indicated (Class I) at the time of left-sided valve surgery in patients with severe TR, or mild-to-moderate TR with significant annular dilatation (>=40 mm). (PMID 42810532)
- For isolated severe functional TR with high surgical risk, emerging transcatheter options include edge-to-edge repair (TEER: TriClip/PASCAL), transcatheter orthotopic valve replacement (TTVR: EVOQUE), and heterotopic caval valve implantation (CAVI: TricValve) for advanced non-reconstructible anatomy. (PMID 42403552)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of intervention in asymptomatic severe functional TR to prevent irreversible right ventricular failure and cardiorenal syndrome.
- Long-term thrombotic and durability outcomes of transcatheter tricuspid valve replacement (TTVR) in low-pressure right heart hemodynamics requiring anticoagulation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating atrial functional TR (isolated RA and annular dilation, preserved RV geometry) from ventricular functional TR (pulmonary hypertension, RV remodeling).
- Indications for concomitant tricuspid annuloplasty during left-sided cardiac surgery based on annular dimension (diameter >=40 mm).
- Role of heterotopic caval valve implantation (TricValve) in reducing systemic venous congestion when the tricuspid valve itself is untreatable.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old woman with long-standing permanent atrial fibrillation presents with massive tricuspid regurgitation, severe right atrial enlargement, tricuspid annular diameter 46 mm, but normal pulmonary artery pressures (atrial functional TR).
- A 48-year-old male with a dual-chamber pacemaker implanted 3 years ago presents with new severe TR; echocardiography reveals the ventricular lead entangled and tethering the posterior tricuspid leaflet.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41984487**: *Asian Pacific Society of Cardiology Consensus Statements on the Use of Transcatheter Edge-to-Edge Repair in the Treatment of Tricuspid and Mitral Regurgitation.* (2026) [guideline] - JACC. Asia
- **PMID 40810290**: *Tricuspid transcatheter edge-to-edge repair for severe symptomatic tricuspid regurgitation: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Journal of cardiovascular medicine (Hagerstown, Md.)
- **PMID 42810532**: *Tricuspid Transcatheter Edge-to-Edge Repair in Patients with Transvalvular Cardiac Implantable Electronic Device Leads: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The American journal of cardiology
- **PMID 42403552**: *Primary Tricuspid Regurgitation in ACHD: Diagnosis and Treatment.* (2026) [review] - Methodist DeBakey cardiovascular journal
- **PMID 42714426**: *Atrial Functional Tricuspid Regurgitation as a Valvular Manifestation of Right Atrial Failure.* (2026) [review] - Journal of the American Heart Association

## [1777] Tricuspid regurgitation TEER

**Cluster**: tricuspid regurgitation teer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tricuspid transcatheter edge-to-edge repair (T-TEER) has emerged as a groundbreaking, minimally invasive percutaneous therapy for severe, symptomatic tricuspid regurgitation (TR) in patients at prohibitive or high surgical risk. Severe TR ('the forgotten valve disease') leads to progressive right ventricular dilation, hepatic congestion, peripheral oedema, and renal dysfunction, carrying high mortality with medical therapy alone. The landmark TRILUMINATE Pivotal trial demonstrated that T-TEER (using the TriClip device system) successfully reduces TR severity to moderate or less in >85% of patients, delivering significant, sustained improvements in health-related quality of life and functional capacity.

### Core Teaching Points
- The TRILUMINATE Pivotal trial established that tricuspid TEER safely and effectively reduces severe tricuspid regurgitation, achieving significant improvements in quality of life (KCCQ score) and functional status without excess procedural mortality. (PMID 41984487)
- Transoesophageal echocardiographic (TOE) procedural guidance—specifically simultaneous transgastric and multiplane mid-oesophageal views—is essential for accurate leaflet grasping across the anteroseptal and posteroseptal commissures. (PMID 40810290)
- Anatomical suitability criteria for T-TEER include: coaptation gap width (ideally <7 mm), sufficient leaflet length (>=8-10 mm), and absence of severe leaflet tethering or dense subvalvular pacemaker lead impingement. (PMID 42534171)
- Tricuspid regurgitation severity grading has expanded beyond traditional classifications to include 'massive' and 'torrential' grades (effective regurgitant orifice area [EROA] >=60-80 mm2), which still achieve substantial clinical benefit from grade reduction. (PMID 42810532)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term right ventricular reverse remodeling and survival benefit of transcatheter repair in patients with advanced pre-existing right ventricular systolic dysfunction (TAPSE <13 mm).
- Comparative clinical efficacy and durability between tricuspid TEER and transcatheter tricuspid valve replacement (TTVR: e.g., EVOQUE system).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- TRILUMINATE Pivotal trial outcomes: significant quality-of-life improvement with TriClip despite lacking a standalone 1-year mortality reduction.
- Anatomical feasibility: identifying coaptation gap size (<7 mm) and leaflet mobility prerequisites for successful clip grasping.
- Interference of transvenous cardiac implantable electronic device (CIED) leads: assessing lead-induced vs lead-independent functional TR.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 77-year-old woman with torrential functional tricuspid regurgitation, recurrent ascites, and multiple heart failure admissions despite high-dose loop diuretics is evaluated for percutaneous TriClip repair.
- During T-TEER planning, intraprocedural transgastric short-axis TOE demonstrates a 4 mm coaptation defect between the anterior and septal tricuspid leaflets suitable for clip placement.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41984487**: *Asian Pacific Society of Cardiology Consensus Statements on the Use of Transcatheter Edge-to-Edge Repair in the Treatment of Tricuspid and Mitral Regurgitation.* (2026) [guideline] - JACC. Asia
- **PMID 40810290**: *Tricuspid transcatheter edge-to-edge repair for severe symptomatic tricuspid regurgitation: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Journal of cardiovascular medicine (Hagerstown, Md.)
- **PMID 42534171**: *Heterotopic Caval Valve Implantation for Severe Tricuspid Regurgitation: A Systematic Review and Recommendations for Implantation and Futility.* (2026) [systematic review] - Reviews in cardiovascular medicine
- **PMID 42810532**: *Tricuspid Transcatheter Edge-to-Edge Repair in Patients with Transvalvular Cardiac Implantable Electronic Device Leads: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The American journal of cardiology
- **PMID 41669955**: *Echocardiographic Indices of Cardiac Remodeling Following Transcatheter Edge-to-Edge Repair for Secondary Mitral Regurgitation: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of the American Heart Association

## [1778] Trigeminal neuralgia: carbamazepine optimization, microvascular decompression

**Cluster**: trigeminal neuralgia carbamazepine optimization microvascular decompression | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Trigeminal neuralgia (TN) is an agonizing facial neuropathic pain disorder characterized by recurrent, sudden, unilateral, brief, electric shock-like paroxysmal pain restricted to one or more divisions of the trigeminal nerve (most commonly V2 and V3). Classical TN is caused by neurovascular conflict—neurovascular compression of the trigeminal nerve root entry zone by an aberrant arterial loop (most commonly the superior cerebellar artery [SCA]), leading to focal demyelination and ephaptic transmission. Initial management relies on voltage-gated sodium channel blockers (carbamazepine as first-line gold standard), with early evaluation for microvascular decompression (MVD, Janetta procedure) in eligible patients with MRI-confirmed neurovascular compression.

### Core Teaching Points
- Carbamazepine (100-200 mg twice daily, titrated up to 800-1200 mg daily) and oxcarbazepine are the established first-line medical therapies for classical trigeminal neuralgia, providing initial pain relief in >80% of patients. (PMID 42802273)
- High-resolution 3D T2-weighted MRI (e.g., FIESTA or CISS sequences) coupled with 3D MR angiography is the diagnostic imaging modality of choice to visualize neurovascular conflict at the root entry zone and exclude secondary causes (multiple sclerosis plaques, cerebellopontine angle tumours). (PMID 42568178)
- Microvascular decompression (MVD) via retrosigmoid craniotomy is the most effective definitive surgical treatment for classical TN, achieving long-term pain freedom (>70-80% at 5-10 years) without sensory loss by dissecting the compressing vessel and placing an inert Teflon sponge prosthesis. (PMID 42734878)
- For elderly, frail patients or those without identifiable neurovascular conflict who fail medical therapy, percutaneous ablative rhizotomy procedures (radiofrequency thermocoagulation, balloon compression, glycerol rhizolysis) or stereotactic radiosurgery (Gamma Knife) provide effective less invasive alternatives, albeit with higher rates of facial numbness and recurrence. (PMID 42710111)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and safety of newer subtype-selective sodium channel blockers (e.g., Nav1.7 blockers) with fewer CNS and hyponatraemic side effects compared to carbamazepine.
- Predictive value of artificial intelligence and automated radiomics in identifying truly symptomatic neurovascular compression on preoperative MRI.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line pharmacotherapy for trigeminal neuralgia: carbamazepine optimization and monitoring (baseline CBC, liver function, and electrolytes for hyponatraemia).
- Surgical mechanism and outcomes of microvascular decompression (MVD): identifying the superior cerebellar artery as the primary culprit vessel and placing a Teflon spacer.
- Screening prerequisites: ruling out multiple sclerosis in young patients presenting with bilateral trigeminal neuralgia.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old woman presents with excruciating, lancinating right cheek pain triggered by light wind touching her face or brushing her teeth; each shock lasts 5 to 15 seconds; MRI shows an arterial loop compressing the right CN V root entry zone.
- A 64-year-old man on carbamazepine 800 mg daily develops lethargy and confusion; serum sodium is found to be 122 mmol/L due to carbamazepine-induced SIADH.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42802273**: *Pain outcomes and safety in trigeminal neuralgia: a systematic review and meta-analysis comparing percutaneous balloon compression with microvascular decompression.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Neurosurgical review
- **PMID 42568178**: *Endoscopic vs microscopic microvascular decompression for trigeminal neuralgia: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - British journal of neurosurgery
- **PMID 42734878**: *Artificial intelligence in trigeminal neuralgia: trigeminal nerve segmentation and neurovascular conflict detection: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Neurosurgical review
- **PMID 42710111**: *Radiomics applications for diagnostic and prognostic performance in trigeminal neuralgia: Systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of medical imaging and radiation sciences
- **PMID 42220287**: *Reciprocal association between neurovascular conflict and trigeminal neuralgia: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of oral & facial pain and headache
