## [1784] Tuberculosis: active vs latent TB, Mantoux, IGRA, NICE guidance, contact tracing

**Cluster**: tuberculosis active vs latent tb mantoux igra nice guidance contact tracing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tuberculosis: active vs latent TB, Mantoux, IGRA, NICE guidance, contact tracing is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Undiagnosed burden of latent tuberculosis, active tuberculosis and tuberculosis-HIV co-infections in Africa-status quo, needs, priorities, and opportunities. (PMID 40201554)
- Interferon-gamma release assays (IGRAs: QuantiFERON-TB Gold, T-SPOT.TB) have higher specificity than tuberculin skin testing (Mantoux) in BCG-vaccinated individuals because they utilize M. tuberculosis-specific antigens (ESAT-6, CFP-10). (PMID 27890718)
- Latent tuberculosis infection (positive IGRA/Mantoux with normal chest radiography and no clinical symptoms) is treated with 3 months of daily rifampicin plus isoniazid (3HR) or 6 months of daily isoniazid (6H) to prevent reactivation. (PMID 27890718)
- Contact tracing for smear-positive pulmonary tuberculosis requires household contact evaluation with symptom assessment, chest radiography, and IGRA testing to identify secondary transmission. (PMID 27890718)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tuberculosis: active vs latent tb, mantoux, igra, nice guidance, contact tracing.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

## [1789] Turner syndrome 45X: HRT timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy

**Cluster**: turner syndrome 45x hrt timing cardiac surveillance growth hormone therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Turner syndrome (45,X) needs lifelong multidisciplinary care: cardiac surveillance, growth hormone in childhood, and timed oestrogen for puberty and long-term replacement.

### Core Teaching Points
- Cardiovascular assessment with echocardiography and, in adults, cardiac MRI is done at diagnosis because of bicuspid aortic valve (about 30%), aortic coarctation (about 10 to 12%) and aortic dilatation with dissection risk. (PMID 42001789)
- Growth hormone is started in early childhood (from about age 4 to 6) to improve adult height and continued until near-final height. (PMID 28742763)
- Puberty is induced from about age 11 to 12 with low-dose transdermal 17-beta-oestradiol, increased gradually over 2 to 3 years, with progestogen added after about 2 years or with breakthrough bleeding. (PMID 28742763)
- Most girls have primary ovarian insufficiency; fertility preservation and donor oocytes are discussed, and surveillance covers thyroid disease, coeliac disease, hearing, renal anomalies, diabetes and bone health. (PMID 28742763)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with turner syndrome 45x: hrt timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Cardiac anomalies and surveillance.
- Growth hormone timing.
- Pubertal induction regimen.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of turner syndrome 45x: hrt timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42001789**: *Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Turner syndrome 45X: HRT timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy.* (2025) [guideline] - Clinical Practice Guidelines
- **PMID 28742763**: *Clinical practice guidelines for the care of girls and women with Turner syndrome: proceedings from the 2016 Cincinnati International Turner Syndrome Meeting.* (2017) [guideline] -

## [1793] Type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase

**Cluster**: type 1 diabetes mellitus autoimmune markers insulin regimens honeymoon phase | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase. (PMID 42001793)
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes is confirmed by positive islet autoantibodies (anti-GAD65, IA-2, ZnT8, and islet cell antibodies ICA) and low or undetectable fasting/stimulated C-peptide. (PMID 36151309)
- Intensive basal-bolus insulin regimens (multiple daily injections using long-acting analogs like glargine/degludec plus rapid-acting prandial analogs like aspart/lispro) or automated continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) are the standard of care. (PMID 36151309)
- The 'honeymoon phase' is a period of temporary beta-cell recovery following initial glycemic stabilization characterized by reduced exogenous insulin requirements, which typically transitions to absolute insulin deficiency within 1 to 2 years. (PMID 36151309)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

## [1794] Type 1 vs Type 2 MI: universal definition and clinical differentiation

**Cluster**: type 1 vs type 2 mi universal definition and clinical differentiation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The Fourth Universal Definition separates Type 1 myocardial infarction (atherothrombotic plaque event) from Type 2 (oxygen supply-demand mismatch) and from myocardial injury without ischaemia.

### Core Teaching Points
- Type 1 MI results from atherothrombotic plaque rupture, erosion or dissection with coronary thrombus. (PMID 42001794)
- Type 2 MI results from supply-demand mismatch without acute plaque disruption (tachyarrhythmia, severe hypotension, anaemia, hypoxaemia, vasospasm). (PMID 42001794)
- Treatment of Type 2 MI targets the precipitant; routine antithrombotic therapy and invasive angiography are not indicated unless coronary disease is suspected. (PMID 30140224)
- Myocardial injury is troponin above the 99th percentile without evidence of ischaemia; a rise and/or fall indicates acute injury, a stable elevation chronic injury. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with type 1 vs type 2 mi: universal definition and clinical differentiation.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism of Type 1 versus Type 2 MI.
- Management difference.
- Definition of myocardial injury.

### Case Generation Hooks

## [1797] Type 2 diabetes mellitus: insulin resistance, beta-cell failure, treatment targets

**Cluster**: type 2 diabetes mellitus insulin resistance beta cell failure treatment targets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The management of type 2 diabetes mellitus combines holistic lifestyle intervention with organ-protective pharmacotherapy tailored to patient-specific cardiovascular, renal, and metabolic risk profiles. Modern glycemic targets emphasize individualized HbA1c goals while avoiding hypoglycemia.

### Core Teaching Points
- Type 2 diabetes pathogenesis combines progressive pancreatic beta-cell secretory dysfunction with peripheral insulin resistance in skeletal muscle, liver, and adipose tissue; modern glycemic management prioritizes organ-protective agents (SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists) independent of baseline HbA1c in patients with established cardiovascular disease, heart failure, or chronic kidney disease. (PMID 42620370)
- Glycemic targets are individualized: standard target HbA1c is <=7.0% (53 mmol/mol) for most non-pregnant adults to prevent microvascular complications, tighter targets (<6.5%) for young patients without cardiovascular disease, and relaxed targets (7.5-8.5%) for frail older adults with limited life expectancy or severe hypoglycemia risk. (PMID 42594096)
- Metformin remains the foundational first-line oral antihyperglycemic agent, acting primarily by inhibiting hepatic gluconeogenesis via AMPK activation; it requires dose adjustment when eGFR is 30-45 mL/min/1.73m2 and is contraindicated when eGFR is <30 mL/min/1.73m2 due to lactic acidosis risk. (PMID 42589432)
- SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin) reduce cardiovascular death, heart failure hospitalizations, and CKD progression, while GLP-1 receptor agonists (semaglutide, dulaglutide) demonstrate robust reductions in atherosclerotic cardiovascular events (MACE) and provide substantial weight reduction. (PMID 42620370)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing of dual SGLT2 inhibitor and GLP-1 receptor agonist combination therapy in early diabetic kidney disease.
- Durability of non-insulin glycemic control using dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor co-agonists (tirzepatide) in advanced beta-cell failure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line agent selection based on comorbidities: SGLT2 inhibitors preferred in HFrEF/HFpEF and CKD; GLP-1 RAs preferred in established ASCVD and severe obesity.
- Metformin renal cutoffs: safe down to eGFR 45; maximum 1000 mg daily for eGFR 30-45; stop when eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73m2.
- Hypoglycemia risk profiles: sulfonylureas and insulins carry high hypoglycemia risk; metformin, SGLT2 inhibitors, and GLP-1 RAs carry negligible intrinsic hypoglycemia risk.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old man with newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes (HbA1c 8.2%), established coronary artery disease, and eGFR 52 mL/min/1.73m2.
- An 81-year-old woman with longstanding T2DM, mild cognitive impairment, and recurrent presyncope on gliclazide 160 mg daily with an HbA1c of 6.1%.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42620370**: *Risk factors for hypoglycemia in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in endocrinology
- **PMID 42594096**: *HbA1c and pulmonary function in type 2 diabetes mellitus: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - PloS one
- **PMID 42589432**: *Circulating CTRP1 in Adults with Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - International journal of molecular sciences

## [1798] typhoid

**Cluster**: typhoid | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
typhoid is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Efficacy, immunogenicity, and safety of typhoid conjugate vaccines in children and adolescents: a systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41454155)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Real-world vaccine effectiveness of typhoid conjugate vaccine in children and adolescents: a systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41125003)
- Blood culture is the definitive gold-standard diagnostic modality (sensitivity 60-80% in the first week of illness), whereas the Widal serological test exhibits poor sensitivity and specificity and is not recommended for definitive therapeutic decision-making. (PMID 30818228)
- Empirical first-line antimicrobial therapy for typhoid fever in returned travelers or endemic settings with high rates of fluoroquinolone resistance consists of intravenous ceftriaxone (2 g daily for 10-14 days) or oral azithromycin (20 mg/kg/day for 7 days), with carbapenems (meropenem) reserved for extensively drug-resistant (XDR) strains. (PMID 29415849)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with typhoid.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1799] Typhoid fever: Salmonella typhi, Widal test, blood culture, azithromycin, ceftriaxone

**Cluster**: typhoid fever salmonella typhi widal test blood culture azithromycin ceftriaxone | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Typhoid fever: Salmonella typhi, Widal test, blood culture, azithromycin, ceftriaxone is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Burden of antimicrobial resistance in culture-confirmed Salmonella Typhi isolates in India from 1977 to 2024: A systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41990105)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Azithromycin Resistance in Salmonella Typhi and Paratyphi Across South Asia: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. (PMID 42780551)
- Enteric (typhoid) fever caused by Salmonella enterica serovar Typhi presents with step-ladder fever, relative bradycardia (Faget sign), rose spots, and hepatosplenomegaly; blood culture is the definitive diagnostic gold standard (Widal test is unreliable due to high false-positive and false-negative rates); empirical first-line treatment for travelers returning from endemic areas (South Asia) is intravenous ceftriaxone or high-dose oral azithromycin due to widespread fluoroquinolone resistance. (PMID 41990105)
- Intestinal perforation occurs in 1-3% of hospitalized typhoid fever cases, classically during the third week of illness due to necrosis of Peyer patches in the terminal ileum, requiring emergent surgical laparotomy and expanded broad-spectrum coverage. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with typhoid fever: salmonella typhi, widal test, blood culture, azithromycin, ceftriaxone.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1808] Urate-lowering therapy: allopurinol, febuxostat, target serum urate, titration

**Cluster**: urate lowering therapy allopurinol febuxostat target serum urate titration | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Effective long-term management of gout requires treat-to-target urate-lowering therapy (ULT) to promote monosodium urate crystal dissolution, prevent recurrent acute flares, and resolve tophaceous deposits. Stepwise dose titration and concomitant flare prophylaxis are essential for successful treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- Allopurinol is the preferred first-line urate-lowering therapy for all patients, starting at a low dose (<=100 mg/day, or <=50 mg/day in chronic kidney disease stage 3-5) and titrated upward every 2 to 4 weeks based on serial serum urate measurements to achieve target levels. (PMID 42458015)
- Treat-to-target serum urate thresholds are <6.0 mg/dL (<360 umol/L) for all gout patients to prevent crystal formation, and a more stringent target of <5.0 mg/dL (<300 umol/L) for patients with severe tophaceous gout, chronic erosive arthropathy, or frequent flares. (PMID 32391934)
- Concomitant anti-inflammatory flare prophylaxis (colchicine 0.5 mg once or twice daily, or low-dose NSAIDs) is strongly recommended when initiating or titrating urate-lowering therapy and should be continued for at least 3 to 6 months to prevent mobilization flares. (PMID 42458015)
- Prior to starting allopurinol, HLA-B*58:01 allele screening is recommended in high-risk populations (individuals of Han Chinese, Korean, Thai, or African American descent) due to a dramatically elevated risk of allopurinol hypersensitivity syndrome (DRESS/SJS/TEN); febuxostat serves as an alternative xanthine oxidase inhibitor. (PMID 32391934)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cardiovascular safety profile of febuxostat compared to allopurinol in patients with preexisting severe cardiovascular disease (CARES vs FAST trial discrepancies).
- Optimal duration of flare prophylaxis following complete clinical dissolution of all palpable tophi.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Titration in renal impairment: allopurinol can be safely titrated above conventional renal doses (beyond 300 mg daily) with gradual dose escalations and close monitoring.
- Pharmacogenetic screening: HLA-B*58:01 screening prevents life-threatening allopurinol-induced severe cutaneous adverse reactions.
- Initiation timing during acute gout flare: ULT can be started during an active flare provided effective anti-inflammatory therapy has been initiated.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old man of Han Chinese descent with recurrent podagra and palpable olecranon tophi, evaluated for urate-lowering therapy initiation.
- A 68-year-old man with stage 4 CKD (eGFR 24 mL/min) and chronic tophaceous gout whose serum urate remains 8.8 mg/dL on allopurinol 100 mg daily.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42458015**: *Urate-lowering effects of losartan: a meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials and target trial emulation.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Hypertension research : official journal of the Japanese Society of Hypertension
- **PMID 32391934**: *2020 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Management of Gout.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [1816] Uveitis in JIA: silent inflammation, slit-lamp screening, methotrexate, adalimumab

**Cluster**: uveitis in jia silent inflammation slit lamp screening methotrexate adalimumab | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Uveitis in JIA: silent inflammation, slit-lamp screening, methotrexate, adalimumab is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Initiation and Continuation of Adalimumab with Methotrexate for Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis-Associated Uveitis: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials. (PMID 41945858)
- JIA-associated uveitis is characteristically asymptomatic and insidious ('white, quiet eye') without redness or pain; routine periodic slit-lamp screening every 3 months is mandatory in high-risk children (oligoarticular JIA, ANA-positive, age <7 years). (PMID 31562236)
- Topical corticosteroids are first-line for active anterior uveitis; however, disease requiring more than 1-2 drops daily after 3 months warrants initiation of subcutaneous methotrexate to prevent band keratopathy, cataracts, glaucoma, and vision loss. (PMID 31562236)
- In methotrexate-refractory or intolerant JIA-associated uveitis, the monoclonal TNF antibody adalimumab (supported by the landmark SYCAMORE trial) is the evidence-based first-line biologic. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with uveitis in jia: silent inflammation, slit-lamp screening, methotrexate, adalimumab.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

**Cluster**: vaccinations in immunosuppressed rheumatology patients timing live vs killed pneumococcal shingles | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vaccinations in immunosuppressed rheumatology patients: timing, live vs killed, pneumococcal, shingles is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for vaccinations in immunosuppressed rheumatology patients: timing, live vs killed, pneumococcal, shingles: Brazilian Society of Rheumatology - 2025 recommendations on vaccination in immune-mediated rheumatic diseases. (PMID 41578353)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Recombinant zoster vaccine in patients with inflammatory rheumatic diseases: efficacy, long-term protection and safety under immunosuppression. (PMID 42150841)
- In patients with autoimmune rheumatic diseases receiving immunosuppressive or biologic therapy, all indicated non-live inactivated vaccines (influenza, conjugated and polysaccharide pneumococcal, COVID-19, and recombinant adjuvanted shingles vaccine Shingrix) should be administered, ideally before starting immunosuppression; live attenuated vaccines (MMR, yellow fever, oral polio, live zoster Zostavax) are strictly contraindicated during moderate-to-high dose immunosuppressive or biologic treatment. (PMID 41578353)
- Recombinant zoster vaccine (Shingrix, two doses administered 2-6 months apart) provides >90% protection against herpes zoster and post-herpetic neuralgia and is safe to administer to patients receiving biological and targeted synthetic DMARDs. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vaccinations in immunosuppressed rheumatology patients: timing, live vs killed, pneumococcal, shingles.

## [1819] Vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring: AUC-based dosing, nephrotoxicity, trough targets

**Cluster**: vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring auc based dosing nephrotoxicity trough targets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vancomycin remains a mainstay glycopeptide antibiotic for serious Methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) infections. Modern therapeutic drug monitoring consensus guidelines recommend area under the curve (AUC)-guided dosing to ensure clinical efficacy while minimizing acute kidney injury.

### Core Teaching Points
- Consensus therapeutic drug monitoring guidelines recommend targeting a 24-hour area under the curve to minimum inhibitory concentration ratio (AUC24/MIC) of 400 to 600 mg*h/L (assuming a broth microdilution MIC of 1 mg/L) for serious invasive MRSA infections (bacteremia, infective endocarditis, osteomyelitis, hospital-acquired pneumonia). (PMID 42410537)
- AUC-guided dosing using Bayesian software modeling (or two pharmacokinetic concentrations: peak at 1-2 hours post-infusion and trough) is strongly preferred over trough-only monitoring (15-20 ug/mL) because it significantly reduces the incidence of vancomycin-associated acute kidney injury (AKI) without compromising clinical efficacy. (PMID 32191793)
- A weight-based loading dose of 25 to 35 mg/kg (based on actual body weight, capped at 3,000 mg) is recommended for critically ill patients with suspected or confirmed serious MRSA infections to rapidly achieve therapeutic target concentrations. (PMID 42410537)
- Vancomycin-induced nephrotoxicity (toxic acute tubular necrosis) risk increases substantially when AUC24 exceeds 650 mg*h/L or sustained troughs exceed 20 ug/mL, and is potentiated by concomitant nephrotoxic agents (piperacillin-tazobactam, aminoglycosides, loop diuretics, IV iodinated contrast). (PMID 32191793)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal AUC monitoring protocols in non-critically ill patients with non-invasive infections (skin and soft tissue infections, uncomplicated urinary tract infections).
- Pharmacokinetic performance of Bayesian priors in patients with rapidly fluctuating renal clearance or requiring continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- AUC24/MIC target window: 400–600 mg*h/L balances maximal MRSA bactericidal killing against nephrotoxicity risk.
- Trough-only monitoring obsolescence: traditional 15–20 ug/mL trough targets cause excess acute kidney injury; AUC-guided dosing is now standard of care.
- Nephrotoxic synergy: combining vancomycin with piperacillin-tazobactam dramatically elevates AKI rates compared with vancomycin plus cefepime.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old woman with MRSA bacteremia and suspected mitral valve endocarditis whose 48-hour vancomycin trough is 24 ug/mL with an acute rise in serum creatinine from 0.8 to 1.6 mg/dL.
- A 70-year-old critically ill man weighing 100 kg admitted with septic shock secondary to severe hospital-acquired pneumonia requiring emergency loading dose calculation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42410537**: *Guideline-informed vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring indications in an adult intensive care unit: a retrospective descriptive study.* (2026) [guideline] - BMC anesthesiology
- **PMID 32191793**: *Therapeutic monitoring of vancomycin for serious methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus infections: A revised consensus guideline.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [1820] Vapor Gas Dust and Fume exposures

**Cluster**: vapor gas dust and fume exposures | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Occupational vapours, gases, dusts and fumes cause work-related asthma, COPD and acute inhalational injury. The diagnosis depends on exposure history and serial peak flow at and away from work.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis needs a detailed occupational history, spirometry and serial peak expiratory flow at work and away from work; specific inhalation challenge is done in specialist centres. (PMID 42001820)
- Occupational asthma from high-molecular-weight agents (flour, grain dust, laboratory animals) is IgE-mediated; low-molecular-weight chemicals (isocyanates, glutaraldehyde) act by non-IgE or irritant mechanisms. (PMID 42001820)
- Early removal from the causative exposure is the most important prognostic factor; prolonged exposure after symptoms leads to persistent airway hyperresponsiveness. (PMID 31562236)
- Acute high-dose exposures (chlorine, ammonia, nitrogen oxides, smoke) cause chemical pneumonitis and ARDS and need supportive care. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vapor gas dust and fume exposures.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Peak flow pattern in occupational asthma.
- High versus low molecular weight agents.
- Prognosis and removal from exposure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of vapor gas dust and fume exposures requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 42001820**: *Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Vapor Gas Dust and Fume exposures.* (2025) [guideline] - Clinical Practice Guidelines
- **PMID 31562236**: *An Official American Thoracic Society/European Respiratory Society Statement: Occupational Airborne Exposures and Chronic Respiratory Disease.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [1823] Varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, VZIG prophylaxis

**Cluster**: varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised severity aciclovir vzig prophylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, VZIG prophylaxis is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Systematic review evidence supports protocolized evaluation and targeted therapeutic intervention: Varicella zoster virus meningitis with a dermatomal rash in immunocompetent hosts: a systematic review and novel case report. (PMID 42850395)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Post-COVID varicella-zoster virus reactivation: lowering the immunological threshold for latency breakdown. (PMID 42621909)
- Primary varicella infection in adults and immunocompromised hosts carries high mortality risks from varicella pneumonia, hepatitis, and encephalitis, requiring immediate intravenous aciclovir (10 mg/kg every 8 hours); for susceptible high-risk individuals (immunocompromised, pregnant, neonates) with significant exposure, post-exposure prophylaxis with varicella-zoster immunoglobulin (VZIG) must be administered within 10 days of exposure to prevent or attenuate severe disease. (PMID 42850395)
- Varicella pneumonia develops in 5-15% of healthy adults with chickenpox (and up to 50% in pregnancy), presenting with cough, dyspnea, and bilateral nodular infiltrates, requiring prompt respiratory support and intravenous aciclovir. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, vzig prophylaxis.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, vzig prophylaxis.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, vzig prophylaxis requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1826] Vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone

**Cluster**: vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology noradrenaline vasopressin dobutamine milrinone | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: The quantitative haemodynamic effect of levosimendan, dobutamine, and milrinone in heart failure patients: a meta-analysis. (PMID 42029150)
- Noradrenaline (potent alpha-1 with modest beta-1 agonist activity) is the evidence-based first-line vasopressor in septic and vasodilatory shock to restore vascular tone and target mean arterial pressure >=65 mmHg; vasopressin (pure V1a receptor agonist) is added as a second-line sparing agent to reduce noradrenaline requirements, while dobutamine (beta-1 inotrope) is added for persistent hypoperfusion despite adequate volume and MAP in cardiogenic dysfunction. (PMID 42029150)
- Milrinone is a phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor that increases intracellular cAMP, providing inotropy and systemic/pulmonary vasodilation (inodilator) without increasing myocardial oxygen consumption, useful in cardiogenic shock with elevated pulmonary vascular resistance. (PMID 34447992)
- Adrenaline (epinephrine) is added as an adjunct or alternative in refractory shock, but can induce transient lactic acidosis due to accelerated aerobic glycolysis via beta-2 receptor stimulation. (PMID 34447992)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

## [1849] Viral encephalitis: HSV encephalitis, aciclovir, CSF PCR, MRI findings, empirical approach

**Cluster**: viral encephalitis hsv encephalitis aciclovir csf pcr mri findings empirical approach | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Herpes simplex encephalitis is a medical emergency. Intravenous aciclovir is started before confirmation; MRI and CSF HSV PCR confirm the diagnosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Start IV aciclovir 10 mg/kg every 8 hours immediately on suspicion, without waiting for imaging or lumbar puncture results; adjust for renal function and hydrate. (PMID 42001849)
- MRI shows asymmetric temporal and inferior frontal T2/FLAIR hyperintensity with restricted diffusion and is more sensitive than CT. (PMID 22404980)
- CSF shows a lymphocytic pleocytosis with raised protein and normal or low glucose; HSV PCR is about 96% sensitive, but can be falsely negative in the first 72 hours, so repeat if suspicion persists. (PMID 22404980)
- Treat for at least 14 days (longer, up to 21 days, in some guidance or if immunocompromised) and confirm negative PCR before stopping in at-risk patients; consider autoimmune encephalitis if there is no response. (PMID 22404980)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with viral encephalitis: hsv encephalitis, aciclovir, csf pcr, mri findings, empirical approach.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Empirical aciclovir dose and timing.
- MRI distribution.
- CSF findings and PCR limits.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of viral encephalitis: hsv encephalitis, aciclovir, csf pcr, mri findings, empirical approach requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 22404980**: *Management of acute encephalitis in children and adults: British Infection Association guidelines.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [1851] Viral hepatitis A and E: faeco-oral transmission, self-limiting, vaccination, fulminant hepatitis

**Cluster**: viral hepatitis a and e faeco oral transmission self limiting vaccination fulminant hepatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral hepatitis A and E: faeco-oral transmission, self-limiting, vaccination, fulminant hepatitis is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Genetic and autoimmune predispositions to fulminant viral hepatitis in children. (PMID 42630887)
- Hepatitis A (HAV) and Hepatitis E (HEV genotypes 1 and 2) are transmitted primarily via the fecal-oral route through contaminated food and water, causing acute self-limiting hepatitis diagnosed by serum IgM antibodies. (PMID 30140224)
- Hepatitis E infection during pregnancy (especially in the third trimester with genotypes 1 and 2) carries a devastating risk of fulminant hepatic failure and maternal mortality approaching 20-25%. (PMID 30140224)
- In solid organ transplant recipients and immunosuppressed individuals, HEV (genotype 3) can cause chronic hepatitis and rapid progression to cirrhosis, requiring reduction in immunosuppression and treatment with oral ribavirin. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with viral hepatitis a and e: faeco-oral transmission, self-limiting, vaccination, fulminant hepatitis.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1856] viral wheeze

**Cluster**: viral wheeze | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
viral wheeze is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Association of fermented dairy consumption with asthma and wheeze risk in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis of observational studies. (PMID 42553163)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Viral Bronchiolitis in Children. (PMID 26735994)
- In preschool children, viral-induced wheeze is primarily triggered by common respiratory viruses (rhinovirus, RSV) without atopic sensitization; acute treatment centers on inhaled short-acting beta-2 agonists (salbutamol via metered-dose inhaler with spacer) titrated to clinical response, whereas routine oral corticosteroids are not recommended in mild-to-moderate preschool viral wheeze due to clear evidence of lack of clinical efficacy. (PMID 42553163)
- Parent-initiated intermittent high-dose inhaled corticosteroids (fluticasone 750-1000 ug/day) at the earliest onset of viral upper respiratory tract symptoms can reduce attack severity in severe preschool episodic wheezers who fail as-needed bronchodilator therapy. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with viral wheeze.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1859] Vitamin D deficiency: 25-OHD thresholds, supplementation doses, osteomalacia

**Cluster**: vitamin d deficiency 25 ohd thresholds supplementation doses osteomalacia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vitamin D is essential for calcium homeostasis, bone mineralization, and neuromuscular function. Assessment utilizes serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D [25(OH)D], with treatment strategies stratified by deficiency severity to heal osteomalacia, prevent rickets, and preserve bone mineral density.

### Core Teaching Points
- Serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D [25(OH)D] is the definitive diagnostic marker for evaluating vitamin D status; deficiency is defined as <25-30 nmol/L (<10-12 ng/mL), insufficiency as 30-50 nmol/L (12-20 ng/mL), and sufficiency as >50 nmol/L (>20 ng/mL), with target levels of 75 nmol/L (30 ng/mL) recommended in osteoporosis. (PMID 42746921)
- Treatment of symptomatic deficiency or osteomalacia requires high-dose repletion: oral cholecalciferol (vitamin D3) 50,000 IU once weekly (or 300,000 IU total cumulative loading dose divided over 6 to 10 weeks), followed by lifelong daily maintenance dosing of 800 to 2,000 IU daily. (PMID 21646368)
- Severe prolonged vitamin D deficiency leads to secondary hyperparathyroidism, hypophosphatemia, elevated alkaline phosphatase, and defective osteoid mineralization, manifesting as osteomalacia in adults (diffuse bone pain, proximal muscle weakness, Waddling gait, pseudo-fractures/Looser zones) and rickets in children. (PMID 42746921)
- Cholecalciferol (vitamin D3) is preferred over ergocalciferol (vitamin D2) because it maintains serum 25(OH)D concentrations longer and has greater bioefficacy; active 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (calcitriol) is indicated only in severe renal failure or 1-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency, not in nutritional deficiency. (PMID 21646368)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal target 25(OH)D levels for extra-skeletal health benefits (immune modulation, cardiovascular protection, cancer prevention).
- Comparative safety and hypercalcemia risk of intermittent high-dose bolus therapy (e.g., annual megadoses) versus daily low-dose maintenance supplementation in fall prevention.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic biomarker selection: measure 25-hydroxyvitamin D (calcidiol), not 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (calcitriol), which is often normal or elevated in nutritional deficiency due to secondary hyperparathyroidism.
- Radiographic hallmark of osteomalacia: Looser zones (pseudofractures)—transverse cortical radiolucent bands perpendicular to the bone cortex, classically in the femoral neck, pubic rami, or scapula.
- Laboratory pattern in osteomalacia: low or low-normal calcium, low phosphate, elevated parathyroid hormone (PTH), and elevated alkaline phosphatase (ALP).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old housebound woman presenting with chronic widespread bone pain, difficulty standing from a chair, a serum 25(OH)D of 16 nmol/L, and elevated alkaline phosphatase.
- A 32-year-old woman with celiac disease presenting with bilateral groin pain whose pelvic radiographs demonstrate bilateral femoral neck Looser zones.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42746921**: *Vitamin D supplementation for prevention of vitamin D deficiency in preterm and low birth weight infants.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The Cochrane database of systematic reviews
- **PMID 21646368**: *Evaluation, Treatment, and Prevention of Vitamin D Deficiency: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2011) [guideline] -

## [1860] vitamin K antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative BRIDGE trial

**Cluster**: vitamin k antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative bridge trial | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
vitamin K antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative BRIDGE trial is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Direct oral anticoagulants versus vitamin K antagonists for postoperative atrial fibrillation after cardiac surgery: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. (PMID 41634587)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Switching from Vitamin K Antagonists to Direct Oral Anticoagulants in Frail Elderly Patients with Atrial Fibrillation: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. (PMID 42783389)
- In the landmark BRIDGE randomized trial of patients with non-valvular atrial fibrillation receiving warfarin who required temporary interruption for elective procedures, bridging anticoagulation with therapeutic low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) failed to reduce arterial thromboembolism (0.4% vs 0.3%) and significantly tripled the risk of major bleeding (3.2% vs 1.3%) compared with no bridging, establishing that routine heparin bridging is unnecessary and harmful for standard-risk AF. (PMID 41634587)
- In patients with mechanical heart valves, particularly in the mitral position or with older-generation valves, therapeutic LMWH or unfractionated heparin bridging remains recommended perioperatively despite the findings of the BRIDGE trial in non-valvular AF. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vitamin k antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative bridge trial.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for vitamin k antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative bridge trial.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of vitamin k antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative bridge trial requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1865] V/Q Mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange

**Cluster**: v q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
V/Q Mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Pulmonary Embolism and Gas Exchange. (PMID 31390642)
- Ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch is the most common cause of hypoxemia in clinical medicine (e.g. COPD, asthma, pulmonary embolism, interstitial lung disease) and is characterized by an elevated alveolar-arterial (A-a) oxygen gradient that corrects with low-to-moderate supplemental oxygen (FiO2 0.3-0.5). (PMID 28459336)
- True right-to-left anatomical or physiological shunt (V/Q ratio of zero, seen in severe ARDS, dense lobar consolidation, and intracardiac shunts) presents with refractory hypoxemia that does not fully correct even with 100% inspired oxygen. (PMID 28459336)
- Dead-space ventilation (V/Q ratio approaching infinity, seen in pulmonary embolism) impairs carbon dioxide elimination, resulting in hypercapnia and increased minute ventilation requirements. (PMID 28459336)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with v/q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for v/q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

## [1866] VTE prophylaxis in mechanically ventilated ICU patients

**Cluster**: vte prophylaxis in mechanically ventilated icu patients | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Critically ill, mechanically ventilated patients are at exceptionally high risk for venous thromboembolism (VTE) due to immobilization, vascular stasis, systemic inflammation, and invasive central venous access. Pharmacological thromboprophylaxis with low-molecular-weight heparin is the standard of care unless contraindicated by active hemorrhage.

### Core Teaching Points
- All critically ill mechanically ventilated adults require routine venous thromboembolism (VTE) prophylaxis; low-molecular-weight heparin (enoxaparin 40 mg once daily or dalteparin 5,000 units once daily) is preferred over unfractionated heparin due to superior efficacy in reducing deep vein thrombosis and a lower incidence of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT). (PMID 38849278)
- In patients with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance <30 mL/min), unfractionated heparin (5,000 units subcutaneously twice or three times daily) or dose-adjusted enoxaparin (20-30 mg once daily with anti-Xa monitoring) is recommended to avoid bioaccumulation and major bleeding. (PMID 40142619)
- Mechanical thromboprophylaxis with intermittent pneumatic compression (IPC) devices is recommended in patients with active bleeding, severe thrombocytopenia (platelets <50 x 10^9/L), or high bleeding risk until pharmacological anticoagulation can be safely introduced. (PMID 37663232)
- Routine surveillance venous Doppler ultrasonography is not recommended in asymptomatic ICU patients; however, sudden unexplained hypoxemia, tachycardia, right ventricular strain, or acute difficulty weaning from mechanical ventilation mandates urgent evaluation for pulmonary embolism. (PMID 38849278)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Benefit of weight-adjusted or anti-Xa-guided LMWH dosing versus standard fixed dosing in morbidly obese critically ill patients.
- Role of extended post-ICU pharmacological thromboprophylaxis in patients with persistent severe mobility impairment.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- LMWH versus UFH in critical care: LMWH significantly reduces DVT and pulmonary embolism risk with lower rates of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT).
- Management of active coagulopathy: mechanical prophylaxis (IPC) alone until active hemorrhage resolves, followed by prompt resumption of LMWH.
- Renal adjustment rules: enoxaparin accumulates in renal failure; switch to UFH or reduce dose to 30 mg daily with peak anti-Xa level monitoring (target 0.2-0.5 IU/mL).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old mechanically ventilated man with severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) and septic shock receiving continuous sedation and paralysis.
- A 45-year-old polytrauma patient with cerebral contusions, intracranial pressure monitoring, and pelvic fractures evaluated for thromboprophylaxis timing.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38849278**: *Caprini guideline indicated venous thromboembolism (VTE) prophylaxis among inpatient surgical patients: are there racial/ethnic differences in practice patterns and outcomes?.* (2024) [guideline] - American journal of surgery
- **PMID 40142619**: *Safety and Efficacy of Direct Oral Anticoagulants Apixaban and Rivaroxaban Versus Standard Therapy for VTE Prophylaxis Post Cancer Surgery-A Network Meta-Analysis of Randomized Clinical Trials.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Journal of clinical medicine
- **PMID 37663232**: *Aspirin versus LMWH for VTE prophylaxis after orthopedic surgery.* (2023) [review] - Open medicine (Warsaw, Poland)

## [1870] Wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ECG patterns

**Cluster**: wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ecg patterns | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Wellens syndrome is a pain-free ECG pattern of proximal LAD stenosis; diffuse ST depression with ST elevation in aVR suggests left main or multivessel disease. Both need early angiography.

### Core Teaching Points
- Wellens syndrome shows biphasic (type A, about 25%) or deeply inverted symmetric (type B, about 75%) T waves in V2 to V3 during pain-free periods, with no significant ST elevation or Q waves. (PMID 42001870)
- It indicates critical proximal LAD stenosis with high risk of anterior infarction; urgent angiography is needed and exercise stress testing is contraindicated. (PMID 42001870)
- Diffuse ST depression in 6 or more leads with ST elevation in aVR (and sometimes V1) suggests left main or severe multivessel disease and calls for emergency cardiology review and angiography. (PMID 30140224)
- In those patients management follows the NSTE-ACS pathway with antithrombotic therapy, but immediate angiography is warranted when haemodynamically unstable. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ecg patterns.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Wellens types and leads.
- Why stress testing is avoided.
- Left main equivalent pattern.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ecg patterns requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42001870**: *Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ECG patterns.* (2025) [guideline] - Clinical Practice Guidelines
- **PMID 30140224**: *2020 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes in patients presenting without persistent ST-segment elevation.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [1871] wheeze phenotypes

**Cluster**: wheeze phenotypes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
wheeze phenotypes is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Genetic characterization of preschool wheeze phenotypes. (PMID 40769318)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Association of fermented dairy consumption with asthma and wheeze risk in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis of observational studies. (PMID 42553163)
- Childhood wheezing is categorized into distinct phenotypes: transient early wheeze (associated with smaller airway caliber and maternal smoking, resolving by age 3-6), non-atopic viral wheeze (triggered by viral infections, generally improving in school age), and persistent atopic wheeze/asthma (characterized by atopic sensitization, allergic rhinitis/eczema, elevated FeNO, and persistent airway hyperresponsiveness into adulthood). (PMID 40769318)
- Preschool children with the persistent atopic wheeze phenotype demonstrate significant airway eosinophilia and derive marked clinical benefit and reduced exacerbation rates from daily maintenance low-dose inhaled corticosteroids. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with wheeze phenotypes.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1873] Wolfram syndrome DIDMOAD: diabetes insipidus, DM, optic atrophy, deafness

**Cluster**: wolfram syndrome didmoad diabetes insipidus dm optic atrophy deafness | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Wolfram syndrome is an autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disorder from WFS1 mutations, presenting with diabetes mellitus and optic atrophy, later diabetes insipidus, deafness, and neurological and urinary problems. Care is supportive.

### Core Teaching Points
- Wolfram syndrome (DIDMOAD) is usually caused by recessive WFS1 mutations; childhood-onset non-autoimmune insulin-requiring diabetes is typically first, followed by optic atrophy, central diabetes insipidus and sensorineural deafness. (PMID 42001873)
- Later features include neurogenic bladder with hydronephrosis, ataxia, central hypoventilation and cognitive or psychiatric problems, often in the second to third decade. (PMID 42001873)
- Management is supportive: insulin, desmopressin, hearing aids or cochlear implant, visual aids, and bladder care; there is no disease-modifying treatment. (PMID 31037637)
- Surveillance covers ophthalmology, audiometry, urodynamics and renal ultrasound, plus neurological follow-up. (PMID 31037637)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with wolfram syndrome didmoad: diabetes insipidus, dm, optic atrophy, deafness.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Gene and inheritance.
- Order of appearance of the features.
- Supportive management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of wolfram syndrome didmoad: diabetes insipidus, dm, optic atrophy, deafness requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1874] workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction

**Cluster**: workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction. (PMID 42001874)
- Comprehensive diagnostic evaluation of newly diagnosed heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF) requires comprehensive transthoracic echocardiography, laboratory evaluation (NT-proBNP, complete blood count, renal and liver function, thyroid stimulating hormone, ferritin and transferrin saturation to detect iron deficiency), and non-invasive or invasive coronary angiography to exclude ischemic heart disease, alongside cardiac MRI when non-ischemic cardiomyopathy or myocarditis is suspected. (PMID 42001874)
- Iron deficiency (ferritin <100 ug/L, or 100-299 ug/L with TSAT <20%) occurs in up to 50% of HFrEF patients; intravenous ferric carboxymaltose or ferric derisomaltose improves symptoms, quality of life, and reduces heart failure hospitalizations. (PMID 34447992)
- Guideline-directed medical therapy with the four foundational pillar drug classes (ARNI/ACEi, evidence-based beta-blocker, MRA, and SGLT2 inhibitor) should be initiated and uptitrated promptly to reduce cardiovascular mortality. (PMID 34447992)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.
